# Brücke: Complete Exhaustive German–English Etymological Compendium

An exhaustive, unabridged reference repository containing every single word, sound shift law, compound calque, false friend trap, and cultural etymology in the Brücke platform database.

> **Database Inventory (100% Unabridged):**
> - **1227** Core Vocabulary Words & Systematic Sound Shifts
> - **32** Morphological Compound Calques
> - **16** False Friend Traps (*Falsche Freunde*)
> - **28** Daily Cultural Etymologies
> - **9** Sound Shift & Grammar Constellations (including the 7 Proto-Germanic Strong Verb Ablaut Classes)

---

## 1. Systematic Sound Shift Laws & Strong Verb Classes

### The Dental Shift (TH → D) (th → d)
- **Phonetic Rule:** English "th" (/θ/ or /ð/) ➔ German "d" ([d])
- **Historical Linguistics:** Every single original Germanic "th" sound systematically shifted to "d" in High German, while English kept the ancient "th".
- **Philological Note:** Around 500–700 AD in the High German Consonant Shift, dental fricatives hardened into voiced dental stops. This gives you an instant key to dozens of core words.
- **Literature Source:** Documented in Joseph Wright's Historical German Grammar as the primary dental hardening of the Second Sound Shift.

### The Stop Shift (D → T) (d → t)
- **Phonetic Rule:** English "d" ➔ German "t"
- **Historical Linguistics:** Where English kept Germanic "d", High German hardened it into "t".
- **Philological Note:** Voiced stops became voiceless. This explains pairs like day ↔ Tag, door ↔ Tür, and drink ↔ trinken.
- **Literature Source:** Complementary to the TH → D shift: as TH took over D, original D shifted to T in High German.

### The Labial Shift (P → PF / F) (p → pf / f)
- **Phonetic Rule:** English "p" ➔ German "pf" (initial) or "f/ff" (medial/final)
- **Historical Linguistics:** Proto-Germanic /p/ shifted to the affricate "pf" at the beginning of words, and to "f" or "ff" after vowels.
- **Philological Note:** Explains apple ↔ Apfel, pepper ↔ Pfeffer, hope ↔ hoffen, and sleep ↔ schlafen.
- **Literature Source:** One of the defining innovations of High German analyzed in Skeat's Principles of English Etymology.

### The Sibilant Shift (T → S / SS / Z) (t → s / ss / z)
- **Phonetic Rule:** English "t" ➔ German "s/ss/ß" or "z" (/ts/)
- **Historical Linguistics:** Proto-Germanic /t/ shifted to sibilant "s/ss/ß" after vowels, and to "z" (/ts/) at the beginning of words.
- **Philological Note:** Reveals water ↔ Wasser, eat ↔ essen, better ↔ besser, two ↔ zwei, and to ↔ zu.
- **Literature Source:** Described by Walter Skeat as the primary dental affrication and spirantization of High German.

### The Velar Shift (K → CH) (k → ch)
- **Phonetic Rule:** English "k" or "c" ➔ German "ch" ([x] or [ç])
- **Historical Linguistics:** Proto-Germanic /k/ softened to the fricative "ch" in medial and final positions.
- **Philological Note:** Explains make ↔ machen, cook ↔ kochen, break ↔ brechen, book ↔ Buch, and week ↔ Woche.
- **Literature Source:** The Ach-Laut [x] occurs after a, o, u; the Ich-Laut [ç] occurs after e, i, ä, ö, ü.

### The Bilabial Shift (V / F → B) (v / f → b)
- **Phonetic Rule:** English "v" or "f" ➔ German "b"
- **Historical Linguistics:** Intervocalic /v/ and /f/ in English regularly correspond to voiced /b/ in German.
- **Philological Note:** Explains give ↔ geben, live ↔ leben, love ↔ lieben, over ↔ über, and seven ↔ sieben.
- **Literature Source:** Proto-Germanic bilabial spirant *β remained a fricative in English but closed to a stop in High German.

### The Palatal & Guttural Link (Y → G & GH → CH) (y → g & gh → ch)
- **Phonetic Rule:** English "y" (initial/medial) or "gh" ➔ German "g" or "ch"
- **Historical Linguistics:** English vocalized palatal "g" into "y" (say, day, yesterday) and silenced "gh" (night, laugh, light). German preserved the consonant as "g" or "ch".
- **Philological Note:** Old English had /j/ and /x/ which became silent or vocalized. German kept sagen, Tag, gestern, Nacht, lachen, and Licht.
- **Literature Source:** Explains why English spelling has silent "gh" letters — they were original German-like gutturals!

### The Latin / Romance Layer (-ate / -ize → -ieren) (-ate / -ize / -ish → -ieren)
- **Phonetic Rule:** English verbs ending in -ate, -ize, -ify, -ish ➔ German verbs ending in -ieren
- **Historical Linguistics:** English verbs derived from Latin or French directly map to German verbs ending in -ieren.
- **Philological Note:** Past participles of -ieren verbs NEVER take the "ge-" prefix (studieren → studiert, reparieren → repariert).
- **Literature Source:** Adopted from Old French -ier in Middle High German as the universal productive suffix for foreign loan verbs.

### The 7 Strong Verb Classes (Ablaut Vowel Harmony) (Ablaut: sing/sang/sung ↔ singen/sang/gesungen)
- **Phonetic Rule:** English irregular strong verbs with internal vowel changes ➔ German strong verbs following the exact same Ablautreihe
- **Historical Linguistics:** English and German share the 7 Proto-Germanic verb classes where tenses are formed by shifting root vowels rather than adding endings.
- **Philological Note:** Joseph Wright's Historical German Grammar categorizes these 7 classes. Knowing "sing-sang-sung" gives you "singen-sang-gesungen", "drink-drank-drunk" gives you "trinken-trank-getrunken", and "drive-drove-driven" gives you "treiben-trieb-getrieben".
- **Literature Source:** Inherited from Proto-Indo-European vowel gradation (Ablaut).

| 289 | **Dom** | cathedral / dome | der (m) | /doːm/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Dom ist alt. *("The cathedral is old.")* | Both from Latin domus (church-house) — true cognates, drifted senses. Trap: der Dom = CATHEDRAL. (Dome: die Kuppel.) [wiktionary] | travel |

| 291 | **fast** | almost / fast | - | /fast/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich bin fast fertig. *("I am almost done.")* | Ancient Germanic 'firmly, almost' — true cognate of fast in its 'firm' sense. Trap: fast = ALMOST. (Quick: schnell.) [wiktionary] | core |
| 292 | **bald** | soon / bold | - | /balt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Zug kommt bald. *("The train is coming soon.")* | Proto-Germanic *balþaz — kin to English bold; the 'daring' became 'soon'. Trap: bald = SOON. (Hairless: kahl.) [wiktionary] | time |

| 294 | **Kaution** | deposit / caution | die (f) | /kaʊˈt͡si̯oːn/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Kaution kostet viel. *("The deposit costs a lot.")* | Both from Latin cautio — true cognates, drifted senses. Trap: die Kaution = SECURITY DEPOSIT. (Caution: die Vorsicht.) [wiktionary] | shopping |
| 295 | **dein** | thy / thine | - | /daɪn/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das ist dein Haus. *("That is thy house.")* | The exact twin of thine (Proto-Germanic *þīnaz with th → d) — the KJV's 'thine' and 'mine eyes' are this word's living family. [wiktionary] | core |

| 297 | **in** | in | - | /ɪn/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich bin im Haus. *("I am in the house.")* | The identical twin of in (Proto-Germanic *in); two-way preposition: motion → accusative, location → dative. [wiktionary] | directions |

| 301 | **zwischen** | between | - | /ˈtsvɪʃn̩/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Der Ball ist zwischen den Büchern. *("The ball is between the books.")* | The twin of between (tw → zw + the same -en suffix): 'by-twain' ↔ 'two-ened'. Two-way preposition. [wiktionary] | directions |

| 396 | **Arzt** | doctor | der (m) | /aːɐ̯tst/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Arzt hilft mir sehr. *("The doctor helps me a lot.")* | From Middle High German arzet, from Latin archīāter (chief healer); no English cognate — memorize this one. [wiktionary] | professions |
| 397 | **Bäcker** | baker | der (m) | /ˈbɛkɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Bäcker backt frisches Brot. *("The baker bakes fresh bread.")* | Agent noun of backen (bake) + -er, Proto-Germanic *bakārijaz — the direct twin of English baker. [wiktionary] | professions |

| 472 | **Käse** | cheese | der (m) | /ˈkeːzə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Käse auf dem Brot ist lecker. *("The cheese on the bread is tasty.")* | Proto-West Germanic *kāsī, borrowed early from Latin cāseus — shared with English cheese. [wiktionary] | food |

| 474 | **Suppe** | soup | die (f) | /ˈzʊpə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Suppe ist sehr heiß. *("The soup is very hot.")* | Borrowed via Middle Low German suppe under French influence — both trace to Proto-Germanic *supô 'broth'. [wiktionary] | food |
| 475 | **Salat** | salad | der (m) | /zaˈlaːt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wir essen Salat zum Mittagessen. *("We eat salad for lunch.")* | 15th-century borrowing from Italian salata — the same Romance source as English salad. [wiktionary] | food |
| 476 | **Gemüse** | vegetables | das (n) | /ɡəˈmyːzə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich koche Gemüse mit Reis. *("I cook vegetables with rice.")* | From Old High German muos 'food, porridge'; English mush is only a possible cognate — memorize this one. [wiktionary] | food |
| 477 | **Obst** | fruit | das (n) | /oːpst/ | `Direct Cognate` | Obst ist gesund und lecker. *("Fruit is healthy and tasty.")* | Proto-West Germanic *obaet; no English cognate — memorize this one. [wiktionary] | food |
| 478 | **Kartoffel** | potato | die (f) | /kaʁˈtɔfəl/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich esse Kartoffeln mit Fleisch. *("I eat potatoes with meat.")* | From Tartuffel, from Italian tartufolo 'truffle', Latin terrae tūber 'earth tuber'; no English cognate. [wiktionary] | food |
| 479 | **Reis** | rice | der (m) | /ʁaɪ̯s/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wir essen Reis mit Hähnchen. *("We eat rice with chicken.")* | Borrowed via Late Latin rīsum, Greek órūza — the same trade-route loan as English rice. [wiktionary] | food |
| 480 | **Kuchen** | cake | der (m) | /ˈkuːxən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Möchtest du ein Stück Kuchen? *("Would you like a piece of cake?")* | Proto-Germanic *kōkô; Wiktionary names only Dutch koek — see Apfelkuchen. [wiktionary] | food |
| 481 | **Zucker** | sugar | der (m) | /ˈt͡sʊkɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich nehme Zucker in den Kaffee. *("I take sugar in my coffee.")* | Via Italian zucchero from Arabic sukkar, Sanskrit śárkarā — the same trade-route loan as English sugar. [wiktionary] | food |
| 482 | **Honig** | honey | der (m) | /ˈhoːnɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Honig schmeckt süß. *("The honey tastes sweet.")* | Proto-West Germanic *hunag — Wiktionary: 'Cognate with English honey'. [wiktionary] | food |
| 483 | **Zwiebel** | onion | die (f) | /ˈt͡sviːbəl/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich schneide eine Zwiebel klein. *("I chop an onion small.")* | Borrowed early from Late Latin cēpulla; no English cognate — memorize this one. [wiktionary] | food |
| 484 | **Tomate** | tomato | die (f) | /toˈmaːtə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Tomate ist rot und reif. *("The tomato is red and ripe.")* | From Spanish/French tomate, Classical Nahuatl tomatl — the same New World loan as English tomato. [wiktionary] | food |
| 485 | **Gurke** | cucumber | die (f) | /ˈɡʊrkə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Gurke ist frisch und grün. *("The cucumber is fresh and green.")* | Borrowed from Old Polish ogórek, Greek angoúrion — cognate with English gherkin. [wiktionary] | food |
| 486 | **Karotte** | carrot | die (f) | /kaˈrɔtə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Karotte ist orange und gesund. *("The carrot is orange and healthy.")* | From French carotte, Latin carōta, Greek kărōtón — the same Greco-Latin source as English carrot. [wiktionary] | food |
| 487 | **Birne** | pear | die (f) | /ˈbɪʁnə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich esse eine Birne zum Nachtisch. *("I eat a pear for dessert.")* | From Vulgar Latin pira, plural of Latin pirum; also the 'bulb' of die Glühbirne. [wiktionary] | food |
| 488 | **Erdbeere** | strawberry (lit. earth-berry) | die (f) | /ˈeːɐ̯tˌbeːʁə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Erdbeere schmeckt im Sommer süß. *("The strawberry tastes sweet in summer.")* | Erde (earth) + Beere (berry) — 'earth berry'; the Beere element parallels English berry. [wiktionary] | food |
| 489 | **Saft** | juice (kin: sap) | der (m) | /zaft/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich trinke Saft zum Frühstück. *("I drink juice at breakfast.")* | Proto-West Germanic *sap 'juice, sap' — matching English sap. [wiktionary] | food |
| 490 | **Frühstück** | breakfast (lit. early bite) | das (n) | /ˈfʁyːʃtʏk/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Frühstück ist um sieben Uhr. *("Breakfast is at seven o'clock.")* | Literally 'early piece (bite)': früh + Stück; NOT the break-fast calque English uses. [wiktionary] | food |
| 491 | **Abendessen** | dinner / supper | das (n) | /ˈʔaːbəntˌʔɛsən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Abendessen ist um acht Uhr. *("Dinner is at eight o'clock.")* | Abend (evening) + Essen (meal) — a transparent compound of known parts. [wiktionary] | food |
| 492 | **backen** | to bake | - | /ˈbakən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich backe einen Kuchen für dich. *("I'm baking a cake for you.")* | Old High German backan — explicit cognate of English bake (see der Bäcker). [wiktionary] | food |

| 494 | **immer** | always | - | /ˈɪmɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich trinke immer Kaffee am Morgen. *("I always drink coffee in the morning.")* | OHG iomēr 'ever + more' — cognate with Middle English 'a mare' (evermore). [wiktionary] | time |
| 495 | **nie** | never | - | /niː/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich habe nie Zeit am Montag. *("I never have time on Monday.")* | MHG nie, OHG nio; thin etymology on Wiktionary. [UNVERIFIED — check] [wiktionary] | time |

| 500 | **früh** | early | - | /fʁyː/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Zug fährt sehr früh. *("The train leaves very early.")* | Proto-West Germanic *frō(ī); only Dutch/Low German cognates listed. [UNVERIFIED — check] [wiktionary] | time |

| 504 | **Stunde** | hour | die (f) | /ˈʃtʊndə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Kurs dauert eine Stunde. *("The class lasts one hour.")* | Proto-West Germanic *stundu — cognate with archaic English stound 'hour'. [wiktionary] | time |

| 506 | **Jahr** | year | das (n) | /jaːr/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich bin zwanzig Jahre alt. *("I am twenty years old.")* | Proto-West Germanic *jār — the twin of English year. [wiktionary] | time |
| 507 | **Moment** | moment | der (m) | /moˈmɛnt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Einen Moment, bitte! *("One moment, please!")* | From Latin momentum — the same Latin loan as English moment. [wiktionary] | time |

| 563 | **Freund** | friend | der (m) | /frɔʏ̯nt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Mein Freund wohnt in Berlin. *("My friend lives in Berlin.")* | Proto-Germanic *frijōndz, 'the loving one' — the exact twin of English friend. [wiktionary] | people |

| 565 | **Dame** | lady | die (f) | /ˈdaːmə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Dame trinkt einen Kaffee. *("The lady drinks a coffee.")* | From French dame, Latin domina — Wiktionary: 'Cognate with English dame'. [wiktionary] | people |
| 566 | **Mensch** | human / person (kin: mannish) | der (m) | /mɛnʃ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Mensch liest ein Buch. *("The human reads a book.")* | Proto-Germanic *manniskaz 'human', built on Mann — English mannish is the cognate form. [wiktionary] | people |
| 567 | **Schule** | school | die (f) | /ˈʃuːlə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Schule beginnt um acht Uhr. *("School starts at eight o'clock.")* | From Latin (ultimately Greek scholē) — the same classical loan as English school; see Schüler. [wiktionary] | people |
| 568 | **Bahnhof** | train station | der (m) | /ˈbaːnˌhoːf/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Bahnhof ist groß. *("The train station is big.")* | Bahn (track) + Hof (yard) — the track-yard; a transparent compound of German parts. [wiktionary] | people |
| 569 | **Flughafen** | airport | der (m) | /ˈfluːkˌhaːfən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Flughafen ist weit weg. *("The airport is far away.")* | Flug (flight) + Hafen (port) — the flight-port; see der Fernseher-style calques. [wiktionary] | people |
| 570 | **Hotel** | hotel | das (n) | /hoˈtɛl/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Hotel ist sehr teuer. *("The hotel is very expensive.")* | Borrowed from French hôtel — the same French loan as English hotel. [wiktionary] | people |
| 571 | **Urlaub** | vacation (twin: leave) | der (m) | /ˈuːɐ̯ˌlaʊ̯p/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich fahre im Urlaub nach Italien. *("I go to Italy on vacation.")* | Middle High German urloup 'permission to be away', kin to erlauben — cognate with English leave. [wiktionary] | people |
| 572 | **wir** | we | - | /viːɐ̯/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wir lernen Deutsch. *("We are learning German.")* | Proto-Germanic *wīz — the very same pronoun as English we. [wiktionary] | people |
| 573 | **uns** | us | - | /ʊns/ | `Direct Cognate` | Er hilft uns. *("He helps us.")* | The accusative/dative of wir — the same West Germanic pronoun as English us. [wiktionary] | people |
| 574 | **jung** | young | - | /jʊŋk/ | `Direct Cognate` | Mein Bruder ist sehr jung. *("My brother is very young.")* | Proto-Germanic *jungaz — the twin of English young (see der Junge). [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 575 | **schlecht** | bad (twin: slight) | - | /ʃlɛçt/ | `y_gh_to_g_ch` | Das Wetter ist heute schlecht. *("The weather is bad today.")* | Proto-Germanic *slihtaz 'smooth, level' — Wiktionary: cognate with English slight. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 576 | **lang** | long | - | /laŋk/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Film ist sehr lang. *("The film is very long.")* | Proto-Germanic *langaz — the identical twin of English long. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 577 | **kurz** | short (kin: curt) | - | /kʊʁt͡s/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Pause ist kurz. *("The break is short.")* | From Latin curtus — the same Latin word as English curt; NOT related to short. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 578 | **leicht** | easy / light (twin: light) | - | /laɪ̯çt/ | `y_gh_to_g_ch` | Die Prüfung ist leicht. *("The exam is easy.")* | Proto-Germanic *linhtaz — English light is the explicit cognate: gh ↔ ch. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 579 | **schwer** | heavy / difficult | - | /ʃveːr/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Tasche ist sehr schwer. *("The bag is very heavy.")* | Proto-Germanic *swēraz 'heavy, grave'; no English cognate — memorize this one. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 580 | **richtig** | correct / right (kin: right) | - | /ˈʁɪçtɪç/ | `y_gh_to_g_ch` | Deine Antwort ist richtig. *("Your answer is correct.")* | recht (right, the gh→ch twin) + -ig — 'right-y', the same root as English right. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 581 | **falsch** | wrong (kin: false) | - | /falʃ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Meine Antwort ist falsch. *("My answer is wrong.")* | Proto-West Germanic *falskī, from Latin falsus — the same Latin line as English false. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 582 | **sauber** | clean (kin: sober) | - | /ˈzaʊ̯bɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Küche ist sauber. *("The kitchen is clean.")* | Ultimately from Latin sobrius — the same Latin word behind English sober. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 583 | **laut** | loud | - | /laʊ̯t/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Musik ist sehr laut. *("The music is very loud.")* | Proto-West Germanic *hlūd; only Old Saxon hlūd compared — the loud link is not on the page. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 584 | **leise** | quiet / soft | - | /ˈlaɪ̯zə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Bitte sei leise! *("Please be quiet!")* | Proto-West Germanic *līs(ī) 'low, soft'; no cognates named — memorize this one. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 585 | **voll** | full | - | /fɔl/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Bus ist voll. *("The bus is full.")* | Proto-Germanic *fullaz — English full is the explicit cognate. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 586 | **leer** | empty | - | /leːɐ̯/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Glas ist leer. *("The glass is empty.")* | Proto-Germanic *lēziz — English leer is the listed cognate, both originally 'empty'. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 587 | **offen** | open (kin: open) | - | /ˈɔfən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Fenster ist offen. *("The window is open.")* | Proto-West Germanic *opan — English open is listed among cognates, kin to auf. [wiktionary] | adjectives |

| 588 | **glücklich** | happy / lucky | - | /ˈɡlʏklɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich bin heute sehr glücklich. *("I am very happy today.")* | Glück (luck) + -lich; Wiktionary: 'Compare English luck' — a relative via Dutch. [wiktionary] | emotions |
| 589 | **müde** | tired | - | /ˈmyːdə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich bin müde und gehe ins Bett. *("I am tired and go to bed.")* | Proto-West Germanic *mōþī; only Dutch moe named — no English cognate, memorize this one. [wiktionary] | emotions |
| 590 | **traurig** | sad | - | /ˈtʁaʊ̯ʁɪç/ | `d_to_t` | Der Film ist sehr traurig. *("The film is very sad.")* | Proto-Germanic *dreuzagaz — English dreary is the cognate, with English d ↔ German t. [wiktionary] | emotions |
| 591 | **wütend** | furious / angry | - | /ˈvyːtənt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Er ist wütend auf seinen Bruder. *("He is angry at his brother.")* | wüten (to rage, the Wut family) + -end; no English cognate — memorize this one. [wiktionary] | emotions |
| 592 | **Angst** | fear / anxiety | die (f) | /aŋst/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich habe Angst vor Hunden. *("I am afraid of dogs.")* | Proto-Germanic *angustiz; the page names no English cognate (anguish/angry not cited) — treat as memorize. [wiktionary] | emotions |
| 593 | **Lust** | desire / pleasure | die (f) | /lʊst/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich habe Lust auf Eis. *("I feel like having ice cream.")* | Proto-Germanic *lustuz — the explicit twin of English lust; in German it just means desire (Lust haben). [wiktionary] | emotions |
| 594 | **froh** | glad | - | /fʁoː/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich bin froh, dass du kommst. *("I am glad that you are coming.")* | Proto-Germanic *frawaz 'quick, glad' — English frolic (via Dutch) is the named cognate. [wiktionary] | emotions |
| 595 | **Liebe** | love | die (f) | /ˈliːbə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Liebe ist wichtig im Leben. *("Love is important in life.")* | Proto-Germanic *lubō — the explicit twin of English love; the noun of lieben. [wiktionary] | emotions |
| 596 | **nett** | nice / kind (kin: neat) | - | /nɛt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Lehrerin ist sehr nett. *("The teacher is very nice.")* | From Old French net, Latin nitidus — Wiktionary: 'Compare English neat', a shared Romance loan. [wiktionary] | emotions |
| 597 | **langweilig** | boring | - | /ˈlaŋvaɪ̯lɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Unterricht ist heute langweilig. *("The class is boring today.")* | Langeweile (long while) + -ig — literally 'long-while-ish'; no English cognate. [wiktionary] | emotions |
| 598 | **nervös** | nervous | - | /nɛɐ̯ˈvøːs/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich bin nervös vor der Prüfung. *("I am nervous before the exam.")* | From Latin nervōsus — the same Latin loan as English nervous. [wiktionary] | emotions |
| 599 | **stolz** | proud | - | /ʃtɔlt͡s/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich bin stolz auf dich. *("I am proud of you.")* | Proto-Germanic *stultaz 'proud, stiff' — English stout is the named cognate. [wiktionary] | emotions |

| 602 | **ja** | yes | - | /jäː/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ja, ich komme gern mit. *("Yes, I am happy to come along.")* | Proto-Germanic *ja; the German section names only Yiddish — the yea link is not on the page. [UNVERIFIED — check] [wiktionary] | survival |

| 604 | **danke** | thanks | - | /ˈdaŋkə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Danke für deine Hilfe! *("Thanks for your help!")* | Ellipsis of ich danke dir — the same danken already known (the think/thank family, th→d). [wiktionary] | survival |
| 605 | **bitte** | please / you're welcome | - | /ˈbɪtə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Bitte, nehmen Sie Platz. *("Please take a seat.")* | Ellipsis of ich bitte (I beg you), from bitten 'to ask'; no cognate supported on the page. [wiktionary] | survival |

| 610 | **tschüss** | bye | - | /t͡ʃʏs/ | `Direct Cognate` | Tschüss, bis morgen! *("Bye, see you tomorrow!")* | CONFIRMED: Low German adjüs, from Dutch adjuus, from French adieu / Spanish adiós — 'to God' worn down to bye. [wiktionary] | survival |
| 611 | **Hilfe** | help | die (f) | /ˈhɪlfə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Hilfe, ich kann nicht schwimmen! *("Help, I cannot swim!")* | Proto-Germanic *helpō, from helpaną (to help, already known) — the ancestor of English help. [wiktionary] | survival |

| 614 | **buchstabieren** | to spell | - | /ˌbuːxʃtaˈbiːʁən/ | `latin_ieren` | Kannst du das bitte buchstabieren? *("Can you please spell that?")* | Buchstabe (letter, 'book-staff') + -ieren — a native root with the Latin-style -ieren verb suffix. [wiktionary] | survival |
| 615 | **freuen** | to rejoice / be glad | - | /ˈfʁɔʏən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich freue mich. *("I am glad / I rejoice.")* | froh (glad) + -en — froh from Old High German frō, Proto-Germanic *frawaz 'quick, glad'; the English relative is frolic, borrowed from Dutch vrolijk 'joy-ly'. | 631 | **Pilz** | mushroom | der (m) | /pɪlts/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Pilz wächst im Wald. *("The mushroom grows in the forest.")* | From West Germanic bulit, an early borrowing of Latin boletus, the porcini mushroom; English bolete is a direct loan of the same Latin word. [wiktionary] | food |

| 636 | **Senf** | mustard | der (m) | /zɛnf/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Senf ist zu scharf. *("The mustard is too sharp.")* | From West Germanic senap, an early borrowing of Latin sinapis, itself from Greek; English keeps the same word only in technical sinap terms and uses mustard instead. | 640 | **Pizza** | pizza | die (f) | /ˈpɪtsa/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wir bestellen eine Pizza mit Käse. *("We are ordering a pizza with cheese.")* | Borrowed from Italian pizza, ultimately from Neapolitan via Medieval Greek; English pizza is the same borrowing. [wiktionary] | food |
| 641 | **Müsli** | muesli | das (n) | /ˈmyːsli/ | `Direct Cognate` | Zum Frühstück esse ich Müsli. *("For breakfast I eat muesli.")* | Borrowed from Alemannic Muesli, a diminutive of Mus, meaning porridge; Mus is cognate with English mush, and English borrowed the word muesli from German. | 642 | **Olive** | olive | die (f) | /oˈliːvə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Pizza mit Oliven schmeckt gut. *("The pizza with olives tastes good.")* | From Latin oliva, partly through Middle High German olive; English olive is the same Latin loan. [wiktionary] | food |
| 643 | **Waffel** | waffle | die (f) | /ˈvafl̩/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich esse die Waffel mit Marmelade. *("I eat the waffle with jam.")* | Borrowed from Middle Dutch wafel; English waffle and wafer come from the same Dutch and Germanic word. [wiktionary] | food |
| 644 | **Huhn** | chicken, hen | das (n) | /huːn/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Huhn legt jeden Tag ein Ei. *("The chicken lays an egg every day.")* | Huhn — from Middle High German huon and Proto-West Germanic *hōn, kin to Dutch hoen and English hen; all from the same Germanic fowl family (PIE *keh₂n-). [wiktionary] | food |

| 647 | **Rindfleisch** | beef | das (n) | /ˈʁɪntflaɪ̯ʃ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich kaufe Rindfleisch für die Suppe. *("I am buying beef for the soup.")* | Rindfleisch — a compound of Rind (cow, from Proto-Germanic *hrinþaz, ultimately PIE *ḱerh₂- head) and Fleisch (flesh); English lost Rind and took French beef instead. [wiktionary] | food |
| 648 | **Lamm** | lamb | das (n) | /lam/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wir grillen Lamm am Sonntag. *("We are grilling lamb on Sunday.")* | Lamm — from Proto-Germanic *lambaz, the exact cognate of English lamb; the silent b is a shared West Germanic quirk. [wiktionary] | food |
| 649 | **Schinken** | ham | der (m) | /ˈʃɪŋkən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich möchte Schinken und Käse, bitte. *("I would like ham and cheese, please.")* | Schinken — originally a shank or thigh, from Proto-Germanic *skinkō; cognate with English shank and dialectal skink, a shin of beef. [wiktionary] | food |
| 650 | **Soße** | sauce | die (f) | /ˈzoːsə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Soße ist zu salzig. *("The sauce is too salty.")* | Soße — borrowed from French sauce, from Latin salsa (salted), so both English sauce and German Soße carry the same Latin salt-word. [wiktionary] | food |
| 651 | **Joghurt** | yogurt | der (m) | /ˈjoːɡʊʁt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Zum Frühstück esse ich Joghurt mit Obst. *("For breakfast I eat yogurt with fruit.")* | Joghurt — borrowed from Ottoman Turkish yoğurt, exactly like English yogurt; standard German uses der, Austria and Switzerland often das. [wiktionary] | food |
| 652 | **Kakao** | cocoa | der (m) | /kaˈkaʊ̯/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Kinder trinken heißen Kakao. *("The children drink hot cocoa.")* | Kakao — borrowed from Spanish cacao, from Classical Nahuatl cacahuatl (cacao bean); English cocoa and cacao share the same Nahuatl source. [wiktionary] | food |
| 653 | **Limonade** | lemonade | die (f) | /ˌlɪmoˈnaːdə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Im Sommer trinke ich gern Limonade. *("In summer I like to drink lemonade.")* | Limonade — borrowed from French limonade, the same drink-word English took as lemonade; the lemon itself traveled from Asia through Persian and Arabic. [wiktionary] | food |
| 654 | **Schokolade** | chocolate | die (f) | /ʃokoˈlaːdə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Schokolade ist für die Kinder. *("The chocolate is for the children.")* | Schokolade — borrowed via Dutch chocolade from Spanish chocolate, from Classical Nahuatl chocolātl; English chocolate comes from the same Spanish word. [wiktionary] | food |
| 655 | **Keks** | cookie | der (m) | /keːks/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich nehme einen Keks zum Kaffee. *("I am taking a cookie with my coffee.")* | Keks — a 1900s borrowing of English cakes, popularized when Bahlsen renamed Leibniz-Cakes to Leibniz-Keks in 1911; northern German uses der, the south often das. [wiktionary] | food |
| 656 | **Torte** | torte, fancy cake | die (f) | /ˈtɔʁtə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Zum Geburtstag backen wir eine Torte. *("We are baking a cake for the birthday.")* | Torte — 15th century, from Middle French tarte and tourte, later reinforced by Italian torta, from Late Latin torta (round loaf); English torte was borrowed back from German. [wiktionary] | food |
| 657 | **Brötchen** | bread roll | das (n) | /ˈbʁøːtçən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich kaufe jeden Morgen frische Brötchen. *("I buy fresh rolls every morning.")* | Brötchen — diminutive of Brot (bread), which is cognate with English bread; both trace to PIE *bʰrewh₁-, the root of brew. [wiktionary] | food |
| 658 | **Toast** | toast | der (m) | /toːst/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich esse Toast mit Käse. *("I eat toast with cheese.")* | Toast — borrowed straight from English toast, with toasten and Toaster as later German offshoots. [wiktionary] | food |
| 659 | **Marmelade** | jam, marmalade | die (f) | /ˌmarməˈlaːdə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wir essen Brot mit Marmelade. *("We eat bread with jam.")* | Marmelade — 16th century, from Middle French marmelade, from Portuguese marmelada (quince jam); English marmalade took the same route. [wiktionary] | food |
| 660 | **Mahlzeit** | meal | die (f) | /ˈmaːlˌt͡saɪ̯t/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Wir essen drei Mahlzeiten am Tag. *("We eat three meals a day.")* | Mahlzeit — Mahl (meal) plus Zeit (time); Wiktionary compares English mealtide, so Mahl is a direct cognate of meal and Zeit of tide, whose t became z in German. [wiktionary] | food |
| 661 | **Getränk** | drink, beverage | das (n) | /ɡəˈtʁɛŋk/ | `d_to_t` | Welches Getränk möchten Sie? *("Which drink would you like?")* | Getränk — a collective built on the old word Trank (drink), the same root as trinken; English drink shows the old d where German shifted it to t. [wiktionary] | food |
| 662 | **süß** | sweet | - | /zyːs/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Der Kuchen ist zu süß für mich. *("The cake is too sweet for me.")* | süß — from Proto-Germanic *swōtuz, PIE *swéh₂dus, exactly cognate with English sweet; the final t shifted to ß and the old w was lost before the rounded vowel. [wiktionary] | food |
| 663 | **sauer** | sour | - | /ˈzaʊ̯ər/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Apfel schmeckt sauer. *("The apple tastes sour.")* | sauer — from Proto-Germanic *sūraz, PIE *súHros, the exact cognate of English sour; the long ū diphthongized to au in both languages. [wiktionary] | food |
| 664 | **bitter** | bitter | - | /ˈbɪtɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Kaffee ohne Zucker ist bitter. *("Coffee without sugar is bitter.")* | bitter — from Proto-Germanic *bitraz, the very same Germanic word as English bitter; German sources tie it to beißen (to bite), the biting taste. [wiktionary] | food |
| 665 | **salzig** | salty | - | /ˈzaltsɪç/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Die Pommes sind zu salzig. *("The fries are too salty.")* | salzig — from Salz (salt) plus -ig; the old t survives in English salt but shifted to z in German Salz, the Sibilant Shift at work. [wiktionary] | food |
| 666 | **Lebensmittel** | groceries, food | das (n) | /ˈleːbənsˌmɪtəl/ | `v_to_b`, `d_to_t` | Wir kaufen Lebensmittel im Supermarkt. *("We buy groceries at the supermarket.")* | Lebensmittel — Leben (life, cognate with live, whose v German closed to b) plus Mittel (means, cognate with middle via the d to t stop shift); literally means of life. [wiktionary] | food |
| 667 | **Appetit** | appetite | der (m) | /ˌapəˈtiːt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich habe großen Appetit auf Kuchen. *("I have a big appetite for cake.")* | Appetit — borrowed via Old French from Late Latin appetītus (striving, desire); English appetite is the very same Latin word. [wiktionary] | food |
| 668 | **Gewürz** | spice | das (n) | /ɡəˈvʏʁt͡s/ | `Direct Cognate` | Diese Suppe braucht noch ein Gewürz. *("This soup needs one more spice.")* | Gewürz — a 14th-century collective of Wurz (wort, herb, root), compared by Wiktionary with English wort; spices are literally the old herbs. [wiktionary] | food |
| 669 | **Menü** | set menu | das (n) | /meˈnyː/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich bestelle das Menü mit Suppe. *("I am ordering the set menu with soup.")* | Menü — borrowed from French menu, the same word behind English menu; its computing sense later came in as a semantic loan from English. [wiktionary] | food |
| 670 | **Farbe** | color; paint | die (f) | /ˈfarbə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Welche Farbe hat dein Auto? *("What color is your car?")* | Proto-Germanic *farwō 'color' (Old High German farawa), from the PIE root *perḱ- 'colored'; no English cognate is listed on the page. [wiktionary] | colors |
| 671 | **violett** | violet (color) | - | /ˌviːoˈlɛt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ihre Lieblingsfarbe ist violett. *("Her favorite color is violet.")* | Borrowed from French violet, the flower name — the page's own derivation; English uses the identical word for the identical flower and color. [wiktionary] | colors |
| 672 | **golden** | golden | - | /ˈɡɔl.dən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Sie hat langes goldenes Haar. *("She has long golden hair.")* | Gold 'gold' + the suffix -en 'made of' — the page explicitly lists English golden as a cognate (Proto-Germanic *gulþīnaz). [wiktionary] | colors |
| 673 | **Jahreszeit** | season | die (f) | /ˈjaːʁəsˌt͡saɪ̯t/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Es gibt vier Jahreszeiten im Jahr. *("There are four seasons in a year.")* | Jahr + Zeit — year-time; Jahr is the twin of English year and Zeit the twin of tide, which originally meant time. [wiktionary] | seasons |
| 674 | **Zahl** | number | die (f) | /tsaːl/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Die Zahl sieben ist meine Glückszahl. *("The number seven is my lucky number.")* | From Proto-Germanic *talō — the entry lists English tale as a cognate, the same tal-root as tell; the initial t became z. [wiktionary] | numbers |
| 675 | **Nummer** | number | die (f) | /ˈnʊmɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wie ist deine Telefonnummer? *("What is your phone number?")* | From Italian numero, from Latin numerus — the same Latin word that gives English number. [wiktionary] | numbers |
| 676 | **Hälfte** | half | die (f) | /ˈhɛlftə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich esse nur die Hälfte des Kuchens. *("I eat only half of the cake.")* | halb + the deadjectival suffix -te — the twin of half; the entry notes the old b ↔ ft alternation was never levelled. [wiktionary] | numbers |
| 677 | **erst** | not until, only | - | /eːrst/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Zug fährt erst um zehn. *("The train does not leave until ten.")* | The twin of archaic English erst, as in erstwhile — the English entry lists German erste as a cognate of the same Germanic superlative. [wiktionary] | numbers |
| 678 | **letzte** | last | - | /ˈlɛt͡stə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der letzte Bus fährt um elf. *("The last bus leaves at eleven.")* | Historically the superlative of lat — the twin of late; the entry lists English latest and last as cognates. [wiktionary] | numbers |
| 679 | **nächste** | next | - | /ˈnɛːçstə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Nächste Woche fahre ich nach Hamburg. *("Next week I am going to Hamburg.")* | The superlative of nah — the twin of next, which was originally nighest, the superlative of nigh. [wiktionary] | numbers |
| 680 | **doppelt** | double | - | /ˈdɔpəlt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das ist doppelt so teuer. *("That is twice as expensive.")* | Past participle of doppeln, borrowed from Old French doble — the very word that became English double. [wiktionary] | numbers |
| 681 | **zählen** | to count | - | /ˈtsɛːlən/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Die Kinder zählen bis zwanzig. *("The children count to twenty.")* | From Proto-Germanic *taljaną, the causative of the tal-root — the same family as English tell and tale; the initial t became z, as in two, zwei. [wiktionary] | numbers |
| 682 | **rechnen** | to calculate | - | /ˈʁɛçnən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wir rechnen im Matheunterricht. *("We calculate in maths class.")* | The twin of English reckon — the entry lists English reckon as its English cognate, both from the PIE root *h₃reǵ-. [wiktionary] | numbers |
| 683 | **Rechnung** | bill | die (f) | /ˈʁɛçnʊŋ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Herr Ober, die Rechnung bitte! *("Waiter, the bill please!")* | rechnen + -ung — the twin of English reckoning; the entry lists English reckoning as a cognate. [wiktionary] | numbers |
| 684 | **Menge** | amount, quantity | die (f) | /ˈmɛŋə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Eine Menge Leute war da. *("A lot of people were there.")* | From Proto-Germanic *managį̄ — the twin of English many, which kept the older vowel. [wiktionary] | numbers |

| 685 | **neu** | new | - | /nɔʏ̯/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Auto ist neu. *("The car is new.")* | From Middle High German niuwe, Proto-Germanic *niwjaz, the exact twin of English new; both go back to the same Indo-European root. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 686 | **schnell** | fast | - | /ʃnɛl/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Zug ist sehr schnell. *("The train is very fast.")* | From Middle High German snel, Proto-Germanic *snellaz, meaning quick and bold; the exact English cognate is the rare word snell, still heard in northern England and Scotland. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 687 | **langsam** | slow | - | /ˈlaŋzaːm/ | `Direct Cognate` | Bitte sprich langsam. *("Please speak slowly.")* | Built exactly like English longsome: lang (long) + the suffix -sam, from Proto-West Germanic *langasam. English lost longsome, but the pieces and the pattern are identical. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 688 | **stark** | strong | - | /ʃtaʁk/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Kaffee ist stark. *("The coffee is strong.")* | From Old High German stark, Proto-West Germanic *stark; the everyday English word stark, as in stark naked, is the very same Germanic word. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 689 | **schwach** | weak | - | /ˈʃvax/ | `Direct Cognate` | Nach der Krankheit war er schwach. *("After the illness he was weak.")* | From Middle High German swach, Proto-West Germanic *swak; the English cousins are the dialectal word swack, meaning nimble or lively, and Scots swack. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 690 | **hart** | hard | - | /haʁt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Brot ist heute sehr hart. *("The bread is very hard today.")* | From Old High German hart, Proto-Germanic *harduz, the exact twin of English hard; both trace to the same Indo-European root meaning strong. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 691 | **fremd** | foreign | - | /fʁɛmt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Stadt war mir fremd. *("The city was strange to me.")* | From Old High German fremidi, Proto-West Germanic *framiþī; English once had the identical word fremd, meaning foreign or strange, now obsolete, and Dutch keeps vreemd. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 692 | **eigen** | own | - | /ˈaɪ̯ɡən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich habe mein eigenes Zimmer. *("I have my own room.")* | From Proto-Germanic *aiganaz, the past participle of the verb meaning to have or own; it is the exact parallel of English own, which descends from the same participle of the same verb. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 693 | **ernst** | serious | - | /ɛʁnst/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Thema ist sehr ernst. *("The topic is very serious.")* | From Old High German ernust, Proto-Germanic *ernustuz; the English cognate is earnest, as in an earnest promise. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 694 | **wichtig** | important | - | /ˈvɪçtɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wasser ist wichtig für die Gesundheit. *("Water is important for health.")* | From the root of Gewicht (weight) plus -ig; the entry itself compares English weighty, which builds importance out of weight with the very same metaphor. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 695 | **interessant** | interesting | - | /ˌɪntəʁɛˈsant/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Film war sehr interessant. *("The film was very interesting.")* | Borrowed from French interessant; it belongs with Interesse and interessieren, and English interesting grew from the same Latin source, so the two are partners rather than twins. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 696 | **lustig** | funny | - | /ˈlʊstɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Clown ist lustig. *("The clown is funny.")* | Lust (desire, joy) plus -ig; Lust is the exact cognate of English lust, from Proto-Germanic *lustuz, though in German the sexual shade is weak and the word still means plain pleasure, as in Lust haben. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 697 | **dumm** | stupid | - | /dʊm/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das war eine dumme Frage. *("That was a silly question.")* | From Middle High German tump, Old High German tumb, Proto-Germanic *dumbaz, the exact twin of English dumb; German smoothed the -mb- cluster to -mm- and kept the old meaning. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 698 | **klug** | clever | - | /kluːk/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Mädchen ist klug und freundlich. *("The girl is clever and friendly.")* | From Middle Low German klok via the Lower Rhineland, Proto-Germanic *klokaz; cognates include Old Norse klokr and dialectal Dutch kloek. Standard English has no cousin of this word. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 699 | **freundlich** | friendly | - | /ˈfʁɔʏntlɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Verkäufer ist sehr freundlich. *("The salesman is very friendly.")* | Freund (friend) plus -lich; the whole compound is documented back to Proto-Germanic *frijondlikaz, matching English friendly part for part, and the -lich suffix is the same element as English -ly. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 700 | **höflich** | polite | - | /ˈhøːflɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Sei höflich zum Gast. *("Be polite to the guest.")* | Hof (court, farm) plus -lich, literally courtly, in the manner of the court; the entry parallels it with Old French cortois, which gave English courteous — polite speech learned at court in both languages. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 701 | **ehrlich** | honest | - | /ˈeːɐ̯lɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Sei ehrlich mit mir. *("Be honest with me.")* | Ehre (honour) plus -lich, inherited from Middle High German erlich and Old High German erlih, built on the old honour root of Proto-Indo-European *h₂eys-. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 702 | **schmutzig** | dirty | - | /ˈʃmʊt͡sɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Küche ist schmutzig. *("The kitchen is dirty.")* | Schmutz (dirt) plus -ig; the entry says to compare English smutty, a word from the same Germanic family of dirty words with the same -y ending. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 703 | **geschlossen** | closed | - | /ɡəˈʃlɔsn̩/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Das Geschäft ist am Sonntag geschlossen. *("The shop is closed on Sunday.")* | Past participle of schließen (to close), from Proto-Germanic *slūtaną, to lock or shut; cognate with Dutch sluiten and English slot, which first meant the bolt or bar of a door. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 704 | **fertig** | ready | - | /ˈfɛʁtɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Essen ist fertig. *("The food is ready.")* | From Old High German fartig, literally journey-ready, from fart (journey), the ancestor of modern Fahrt; the root belongs with the Germanic travel word behind English fare, and Dutch has the parallel vaardig. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 705 | **sicher** | safe | - | /ˈzɪçɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Passwort ist sicher. *("The password is secure.")* | Borrowed very early, already in Old High German, from Latin securus (free from care); that is the same Latin word that later reached English as secure and sure. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 706 | **gefährlich** | dangerous | - | /ɡəˈfɛːɐ̯lɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Feuer ist gefährlich. *("The fire is dangerous.")* | Gefahr (danger) plus -lich; Gefahr goes back to Proto-Germanic *fērō, which is also the ancestor of English fear — German kept the old meaning danger, while English drifted to the feeling that danger produces. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 707 | **frei** | free | - | /fʁaɪ̯/ | `Direct Cognate` | Morgen bin ich frei. *("Tomorrow I am free.")* | From Old High German frī, Proto-West Germanic *frī, the exact twin of English free; both go back to the Indo-European root meaning beloved, the same root that gave German Freund and English friend. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 708 | **still** | quiet | - | /ʃtɪl/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Bibliothek ist sehr still. *("The library is very quiet.")* | From Middle High German stille, Proto-West Germanic *stillī; English still is the same word, and both kept the sense of motionless and silent. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 709 | **satt** | full | - | /zat/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Kind ist satt. *("The child is full.")* | From Old High German sat, Proto-Germanic *sadaz, meaning sated or full; the exact cognate is English sad, which originally meant sated before it drifted to its modern sorrowful sense. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 710 | **gleich** | same | - | /ɡlaɪ̯ç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Zwillinge sehen gleich aus. *("The twins look alike.")* | From Proto-Germanic *galīkaz, having the same form, built from ga- plus the ancestor of -like; the English twin is alike, and the suffix inside it is the same element as English -like and -ly. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 711 | **mutig** | brave | - | /ˈmuːtɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Junge war mutig. *("The boy was brave.")* | Mut (courage) plus -ig, Proto-Germanic *mōdagaz, whose English twin is moody — in Old English moody still meant brave and only later slid to gloomy. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 712 | **lecker** | delicious | - | /ˈlɛkɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Kuchen schmeckt lecker. *("The cake tastes delicious.")* | From Middle High German lecker, tied to the verb lecken, to lick, Proto-West Germanic *likkōn; the entry points to English lick as the relative, so the word family is literally about licking. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 713 | **einfach** | simple; easy | - | /ˈʔaɪ̯nfax/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Aufgabe ist ganz einfach. *("The task is quite simple.")* | ein 'one' plus the suffix -fach '-fold': literally onefold, the exact comparison Wiktionary itself draws with English onefold. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 714 | **schwierig** | difficult | - | /ˈʃviːrɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Diese Frage ist schwierig. *("This question is difficult.")* | From Middle High German swiric 'full of boils', built on Schwäre 'ulcer'; Wiktionary warns it is often connected to schwer but is unrelated. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 715 | **weniger** | less; fewer | - | /ˈveːnɪɡɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich trinke weniger Kaffee. *("I drink less coffee.")* | Comparative of wenig, built on the Proto-Germanic root *wainagaz that Wiktionary ties to English woe: from woe-like, miserable to few and less. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 716 | **ruhig** | quiet; calm | - | /ˈʁuː.ɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Bleib ruhig und atme tief. *("Stay calm and breathe deeply.")* | From Ruhe 'rest, calm' + -ig; Middle High German ruowec meant quiet. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 717 | **ängstlich** | anxious; fearful | - | /ˈɛŋstlɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Er ist ängstlich vor Hunden. *("He is anxious around dogs.")* | From Angst 'fear, dread' + the suffix -lich — literally angst-like, inclined to fear. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 718 | **neugierig** | curious; nosy | - | /ˈnɔʏ̯ˌɡiːʁɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Katze ist sehr neugierig. *("The cat is very curious.")* | A Middle Low German formation of neu 'new' + gierig 'greedy' — greedy for the new; Dutch nieuwsgierig and Norwegian nysgjerrig are built the same way. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 719 | **toll** | great; awesome | - | /tɔl/ | `d_to_t` | Die Party gestern war toll. *("The party yesterday was great.")* | Proto-Germanic *dulaz 'foolish, dazed' — the same word as English dull, which etymonline lists beside German toll; German hardened d to t and the sense flipped from mad to great. [wiktionary] [etymonline] | adjectives |
| 720 | **wunderbar** | wonderful | - | /ˈvʊndəʁˌbaːʁ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Wetter ist heute wunderbar. *("The weather is wonderful today.")* | Wunder 'wonder' + -bar '-able' — literally wonder-able; Wunder is the twin of English wonder (Proto-Germanic *wundran, per etymonline). [wiktionary] [etymonline] | adjectives |
| 721 | **schrecklich** | terrible; awful | - | /ˈʃʁɛklɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Sturm war schrecklich. *("The storm was terrible.")* | From Schreck 'fright, shock' + the suffix -lich, first attested in Late Middle High German — literally frightful. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 722 | **schlimm** | bad; serious | - | /ʃlɪm/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Verletzung ist nicht schlimm. *("The injury is not serious.")* | Proto-Germanic *slimbaz 'crooked, oblique' — the page lists English slim as a cognate; schlimm slid from crooked to bad, and Middle Dutch slim also meant bad. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 723 | **fröhlich** | cheerful; merry | - | /ˈfʁøːlɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Sie lacht immer fröhlich. *("She always laughs cheerfully.")* | Inherited from Proto-Germanic *frawalīkaz: froh 'cheerful' + the suffix -lich. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 724 | **viel** | much; many | - | /fiːl/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wir haben viel Spaß. *("We are having a lot of fun.")* | Proto-Germanic *felu 'many', from PIE *pleh₁- — English once had the twin word fele 'many', the very entry Wiktionary points to. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 725 | **wenig** | little; few | - | /ˈveːnɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich habe wenig Zeit. *("I have little time.")* | Proto-Germanic *wainagaz 'miserable', its vowel blended with Old High German wē, akin to English woe: the woe-word came to mean little, few. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 726 | **ordentlich** | tidy; orderly | - | /ˈɔʁdəntlɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Sein Zimmer ist immer ordentlich. *("His room is always tidy.")* | From ordnen 'to put in order' + -lich; Ordnung 'order' is the same family. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 727 | **breit** | broad; wide | - | /bʁaɪ̯t/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Straße ist sehr breit. *("The street is very wide.")* | Proto-Germanic *braidaz — the exact twin of English broad (Old English brād), with German breit named as its cognate by etymonline. [wiktionary] [etymonline] | adjectives |
| 728 | **schmal** | narrow | - | /ʃmaːl/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Weg ist schmal und steil. *("The path is narrow and steep.")* | Proto-Germanic *smalaz 'narrow, small' — the page lists English small as the direct cognate; German kept the narrow sense. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 729 | **wild** | wild | - | /vɪlt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Fluss ist hier wild. *("The river is wild here.")* | Proto-Germanic *wilþijaz — the exact twin of English wild; the English entry on the same page names German wild as its cognate. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 730 | **sanft** | soft; gentle | - | /zanft/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Musik ist sanft und leise. *("The music is gentle and quiet.")* | West Germanic *samft(ī) — the page lists soft as the only English cognate; the root may reach back to *sem- 'one'. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 731 | **faul** | lazy; rotten | - | /faʊ̯l/ | `Direct Cognate` | Mein Bruder ist heute faul. *("My brother is lazy today.")* | Proto-Germanic *fūlaz 'rotten, putrid' — the twin of English foul, which the page lists as a cognate; German diphthongized the vowel, and the sense slid from rotten to lazy, idleness as moral rot. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 732 | **seltsam** | strange; odd | - | /ˈzɛltˌzaːm/ | `d_to_t` | Der Film war sehr seltsam. *("The film was very strange.")* | Middle High German seltsām, built with the suffix -sam, which the page glosses as English -some; English seldom is listed as a cognate, German having hardened d to t in the root seld-. [wiktionary] | adjectives |
| 733 | **möglich** | possible | - | /ˈmøːklɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ist eine Reparatur noch möglich? *("Is a repair still possible?")* | From mögen 'to be able' + -lich: literally may-ly, what may happen — mögen goes back to the same PIE root *megʰ- that produced English may. [wiktionary] [etymonline] | adjectives |
| 734 | **Norden** | north | der (m) | /ˈnɔrdən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Im Norden regnet es oft. *("In the north it often rains.")* | The compass noun from Nord, the twin of north; used with im for in the north. [wiktionary] | directions |
| 735 | **Süden** | south | der (m) | /ˈzyːdən/ | `th_to_d` | Im Süden ist die Sonne stärker. *("In the south the sun is stronger.")* | From Middle Low German süden, ultimately Proto-Germanic *sunþanē — the old noun *sunþrą gave both English south and German Süd (th → d). [wiktionary] | directions |
| 736 | **Osten** | east | der (m) | /ˈɔstən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Wind kommt aus dem Osten. *("The wind comes from the east.")* | From Old High German ostan, from Proto-Germanic *austrą — the same root as English east, ultimately from the dawn root *ausos, as in Latin aurora. [wiktionary] | directions |
| 737 | **Westen** | west | der (m) | /ˈvɛstən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Sonne geht im Westen unter. *("The sun sets in the west.")* | The compass noun from West, the twin of west; used with im for in the west. [wiktionary] | directions |
| 738 | **hinten** | at the back | - | /ˈhɪntən/ | `d_to_t` | Die Toilette ist hinten links. *("The toilet is at the back on the left.")* | From Proto-Germanic *hindanē — the same hind root as English behind and hind; German shifted d to t (the entry points to hinter for the consonantism). [wiktionary] | directions |
| 739 | **drüben** | over there | - | /ˈdryːbən/ | `v_to_b` | Drüben ist die Post. *("The post office is over there.")* | From a rare üben, a variant of über formed by the analogy of unter/unten and ober/oben — literally over yonder; über is the twin of over. [wiktionary] | directions |
| 740 | **gegenüber** | opposite | - | /ɡeːɡŋ̍ˈʔyːbɐ/ | `v_to_b` | Der Supermarkt ist gegenüber der Bank. *("The supermarket is opposite the bank.")* | gegen + über — literally over-against; über is the twin of English over (both from PIE *upér) and gegen the twin of against. [wiktionary] | directions |
| 741 | **entlang** | along | - | /ɛntˈlaŋ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wir gehen den Fluss entlang. *("We walk along the river.")* | Borrowed from Middle Low German entlanc, essentially in lang — the lang element is the twin of English long, so it works just like along. [wiktionary] | directions |
| 742 | **gegen** | against | - | /ˈɡeːɡən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Spiel beginnt gegen drei Uhr. *("The game starts at about three o'clock.")* | The twin of English against — both from Proto-Germanic *gagin; the entry lists against as a cognate. [wiktionary] | directions |
| 743 | **Person** | person | die (f) | /pɛrˈzoːn/ | `Direct Cognate` | Diese Person kenne ich nicht. *("I do not know this person.")* | From Middle High German persōn, the same Latin loan as English person. [wiktionary] | people |
| 744 | **Leute** | people | die (f) | /ˈlɔɪtə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Viele Leute warten am Bahnhof. *("Many people are waiting at the station.")* | Proto-Germanic *liudiz, kin to the Old English leode (men, people); German keeps the plural-only collective with the standard article die Leute. [wiktionary] | people |
| 745 | **Gast** | guest | der (m) | /ɡast/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wir haben heute Gäste zum Essen. *("We have guests for dinner today.")* | From Proto-Germanic *gastiz, the exact twin of English guest; Latin hostis, enemy, shows the older guest-host pair. [wiktionary] | people |
| 746 | **Feind** | enemy | der (m) | /faɪ̯nt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Er ist mein größter Feind. *("He is my biggest enemy.")* | From Proto-Germanic *fijandz, the hating one, the exact twin of English fiend; both are the participle of the same hate verb. [wiktionary] | people |
| 747 | **Kollege** | colleague | der (m) | /kɔˈleːɡə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Mein Kollege kommt morgen ins Büro. *("My colleague is coming to the office tomorrow.")* | From Latin collega, the same loan that gives English colleague. [wiktionary] | people |
| 748 | **Held** | hero | der (m) | /hɛlt/ | `th_to_d` | Der Held rettet die Stadt. *("The hero saves the city.")* | From Proto-West Germanic *haliþ, hero, cognate with Old English hæleþ, a relative of the English health and hale family; the final th hardened to d. [wiktionary] | people |

| 749 | **Möbel** | furniture | das (n) | /ˈmøːbəl/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Möbel sind neu. *("The furniture is new.")* | Borrowed in the 15th century from Middle French meuble, from Latin mobilis, meaning movable; English mobile comes from the same Latin adjective. [wiktionary] | home |
| 750 | **Wohnzimmer** | living room | das (n) | /ˈvoːnˌt͡sɪmɐ/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Wir sitzen im Wohnzimmer. *("We are sitting in the living room.")* | Compound of wohnen and Zimmer; Zimmer corresponds to English timber via the t to z shift. [wiktionary] | home |
| 751 | **Schlafzimmer** | bedroom | das (n) | /ˈʃlaːfˌt͡sɪmɐ/ | `p_to_pf_f` | Das Kind schläft im Schlafzimmer. *("The child sleeps in the bedroom.")* | Compound of Schlaf and Zimmer; schlafen corresponds to English sleep, with the medial p appearing as f in German. [wiktionary] | home |
| 752 | **Badezimmer** | bathroom | das (n) | /ˈbaːdəˌt͡sɪmɐ/ | `th_to_d` | Das Badezimmer ist sauber. *("The bathroom is clean.")* | Compound of baden and Zimmer; Bad corresponds to English bath via the th to d shift. [wiktionary] | home |
| 753 | **Kinderzimmer** | children's room | das (n) | /ˈkɪndɐˌt͡sɪmɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Kind spielt im Kinderzimmer. *("The child plays in the children's room.")* | Compound of Kind and Zimmer with linking er; Kind is cognate with English kin, and Zimmer with English timber. [wiktionary] | home |
| 754 | **Esszimmer** | dining room | das (n) | /ˈɛsˌt͡sɪmɐ/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Das Essen ist im Esszimmer. *("The meal is in the dining room.")* | Compound of essen and Zimmer; essen corresponds to English eat via the t to ss shift. [wiktionary] | home |
| 755 | **Flur** | hallway | der (m) | /ˈfluːr/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Schuhe stehen im Flur. *("The shoes are standing in the hallway.")* | From Proto-Germanic floraz; English floor is a direct cognate that kept the old sense, while German Flur narrowed to mean hallway. [wiktionary] | home |
| 756 | **Keller** | basement | der (m) | /ˈkɛlɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Fahrräder stehen im Keller. *("The bicycles are standing in the basement.")* | From Old High German kellari, from West Germanic kellari, an early loan of Late Latin cellarium; English cellar is the same Latin word through Old French. [wiktionary] | home |
| 757 | **Dachboden** | attic | der (m) | /ˈdaxˌboːdən/ | `th_to_d`, `d_to_t` | Der Koffer ist auf dem Dachboden. *("The suitcase is in the attic.")* | Compound of Dach and Boden, literally roof floor; Dach corresponds to English thatch via the th to d shift, and Boden to English bottom via the d to t shift. [wiktionary] | home |
| 758 | **Etage** | floor, storey | die (f) | /eˈtaːʒə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wir wohnen in der dritten Etage. *("We live on the third floor.")* | Borrowed from French étage; English stage is a doublet from the same Old French word estage. [wiktionary] | home |
| 759 | **Treppe** | stairs | die (f) | /ˈtʁɛpə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Kinder laufen die Treppe hoch. *("The children run up the stairs.")* | From West Germanic trappa, from the PIE root dreb, meaning to tread or trample; the same stepping root underlies English tread and trip. | 760 | **Aufzug** | elevator | der (m) | /ˈʔaʊ̯ftsuːk/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Der Aufzug ist kaputt. *("The elevator is broken.")* | From Middle High German ufzuc, a verbal noun of aufziehen, meaning to draw or lift up; the Zug element belongs to the ziehen root cognate with English tow, the t appearing as z in German. [wiktionary] | home |
| 761 | **Haustür** | front door | die (f) | /ˈhaʊ̯styːɐ̯/ | `d_to_t` | Der Schlüssel steckt in der Haustür. *("The key is in the front door.")* | Compound of Haus and Tür; Tür corresponds to English door via the d to t shift, the textbook pair of the dental shift. [wiktionary] | home |
| 762 | **Kissen** | pillow | das (n) | /ˈkɪsən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Kissen ist zu weich. *("The pillow is too soft.")* | From West Germanic kussin, an early borrowing of Latin culcita, meaning bolster or quilted cushion; English quilt is a later French loan of the same Latin word. | 765 | **Kamm** | comb | der (m) | /kam/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich brauche einen Kamm für die Haare. *("I need a comb for my hair.")* | From Proto-Germanic kambaz, from PIE gombhos, meaning tooth; English comb is a direct cognate, and both keep the old idea that a comb is full of teeth. [wiktionary] | home |
| 766 | **Klingel** | doorbell | die (f) | /ˈklɪŋl̩/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Klingel läutet an der Tür. *("The doorbell is ringing at the door.")* | From the verb klingen, meaning to ring or sound; the same Germanic kling root underlies English clink. [wiktionary] | home |
| 767 | **Schloss** | lock | das (n) | /ʃlɔs/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Der Schlüssel passt zum Schloss. *("The key fits the lock.")* | From Old High German sloz, ablaut to the verb schließen, meaning to close or lock; English slot and Dutch slot are cognates, the English t corresponding to German ss. | 768 | **Herd** | stove | der (m) | /heːrt/ | `th_to_d` | Der Topf steht auf dem Herd. *("The pot is on the stove.")* | From West Germanic herth; English hearth is a direct cognate, the English th corresponding to German d. [wiktionary] | home |
| 769 | **Ofen** | oven | der (m) | /ˈoːfən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Kuchen ist noch im Ofen. *("The cake is still in the oven.")* | From West Germanic ofn; English oven is a direct cognate from the same Germanic word, differing only in the vowel. [wiktionary] | home |
| 770 | **Waschmaschine** | washing machine | die (f) | /ˈvaʃmaˌʃiːnə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Waschmaschine läuft im Keller. *("The washing machine is running in the basement.")* | Compound of waschen and Maschine; waschen is a direct cognate of English wash, and Maschine matches English machine, both from Latin machina. | 771 | **Mikrowelle** | microwave | die (f) | /ˈmiːkʁoˌvɛlə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Suppe ist in der Mikrowelle. *("The soup is in the microwave.")* | Compound of the Greek loan prefix mikro- and Welle, meaning wave; Mikro- is the same Greek element as English micro-. [wiktionary] | home |
| 772 | **Teller** | plate | der (m) | /ˈtɛlɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Teller ist leer. *("The plate is empty.")* | From Middle French tailloir, meaning cutting board, from the Old French cutting verb tailler; English tailor descends from the same Old French verb. [wiktionary] | home |

[wiktionary] | home |

| 777 | **Flasche** | bottle | die (f) | /ˈflaʃə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Flasche Wasser kostet zwei Euro. *("The bottle of water costs two euros.")* | From Proto-Germanic flasko, from a root meaning to plait or braid, since bottles were once wrapped in straw; English flask is a direct cognate. | 782 | **Dose** | tin, can | die (f) | /ˈdoːzə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Dose steht im Küchenschrank. *("The can is in the kitchen cabinet.")* | Probably from Latin dosis via Middle Low German dose; the sense moved from a dose of medicine to the container that holds it, and English dose is the same Latin word. | 785 | **Regenbogen** | rainbow | der (m) | /ˈʁeːɡənˌboːɡən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Nach dem Regen sehen wir einen Regenbogen. *("After the rain we see a rainbow.")* | Regen (rain) plus Bogen (bow — twin of English bow); the page lists English rainbow as the parallel cognate — the exact same compound. [wiktionary] | weather |

I am making tea.")* | From Proto-Germanic *freusaną — the page names English freeze as a cognate; the strong past fror, gefroren mirrors froze, frozen. [wiktionary] | weather |

English twig is the listed cognate, keeping the t where German shifted to z. [wiktionary] | nature |

| 875 | **Buche** | beech | die (f) | /ˈbuːxə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Buche ist hoch und alt. *("The beech is tall and old.")* | From Old High German buohha, from Proto-West Germanic *bōku and *bōkijā. English beech is the listed cognate; the entry also notes a possible link to Buch, book. [wiktionary] | nature |
| 876 | **Birke** | birch | die (f) | /ˈbɪrkə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Die Birke hat eine weiße Rinde. *("The birch has white bark.")* | From Old High German birka, from Proto-West Germanic *birkijā. English birch is the listed cognate. [wiktionary] | nature |

| 877 | **auch** | also; too | - | /aʊ̯x/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich komme auch morgen. *("I am coming tomorrow too.")* | Proto-Germanic *auk — the same word as the archaic English eke 'also', as in to eke out; English let it fade, German kept auch. [wiktionary] | core |
| 878 | **noch** | still; yet | - | /nɔx/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich habe noch Zeit. *("I still have time.")* | Proto-Germanic *nuh, from PIE *nu 'now' plus *-kʷe 'and, also' — literally and-now; the page lists no English cognate, but that first piece is the very 'now' element. [wiktionary] | core |
| 879 | **schon** | already | - | /ʃoːn/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Zug ist schon weg. *("The train is already gone.")* | Originally the adverb of schön 'beautiful' (Middle High German schōne 'beautifully'); it slid to already, and the page compares English pretty making the same journey. [wiktionary] | core |
| 880 | **nur** | only; just | - | /nuːɐ̯/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich will nur helfen. *("I only want to help.")* | Contracted from Old High German niwāri, literally were it not (ni 'not' + wāri 'were'); Old English had the same word nǣre — from if-not to only. [wiktionary] | core |
| 881 | **sehr** | very | - | /zeːr/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das Essen ist sehr gut. *("The food is very good.")* | Old High German sēr 'injured, painful', from West Germanic *sair — the same word as English sore; both drifted from painfully to very, as English sorely shows. [wiktionary] | core |
| 882 | **gerne** | gladly; with pleasure | - | /ˈɡɛrnə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich spiele gerne Fußball. *("I like playing football.")* | Variant of gern, from Proto-Germanic *gernaz 'eager, willing' — the same word as English yearn (Middle English yern, listed on the page); ich spiele gerne is literally I play yearningly. | 883 | **selten** | rare; seldom | - | /ˈzɛltən/ | `d_to_t` | Ich esse selten Fleisch. *("I seldom eat meat.")* | West Germanic *seldanā — the twin of English seldom, which the page lists as a cognate; German hardened d into t (seld- to selt-). [wiktionary] | core |
| 884 | **dann** | then | - | /dan/ | `th_to_d` | Erst essen, dann schlafen. *("First eat, then sleep.")* | The twin of English then: West Germanic *þan, which the page labels cognate with English then; English kept the th, German shifted it to d. [wiktionary] | core |
| 885 | **vorher** | beforehand | - | /ˈfoːɐ̯.heːɐ̯/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich habe vorher gegessen. *("I ate beforehand.")* | A univerbation of vor 'before' + her 'ago, hither' — literally before-here, an everyday adverb built from two ancient words. [wiktionary] | core |
| 886 | **wirklich** | really; actual | - | /ˈvɪʁklɪç/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das ist wirklich schön. *("That is really beautiful.")* | From wirken 'to act, take effect' + -lich: originally in a working, effective way, hence really. [wiktionary] | core |
| 887 | **bestimmt** | definitely; certain | - | /bəˈʃtɪmt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Er kommt bestimmt morgen. *("He is definitely coming tomorrow.")* | The past participle of bestimmen 'to determine': determined, fixed — hence the particle sense certainly. [wiktionary] | core |
| 888 | **zusammen** | together | - | /tsuˈzamən/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Wir kochen zusammen. *("We are cooking together.")* | zu 'to' + sammen 'together', from PIE *sem- 'one': Middle English had the same compound tosamen and obsolete English sam meant together — the classic English t to German z shift sits right in zu. [wiktionary] | core |
| 889 | **allein** | alone | - | /aˈlaɪn/ | `Direct Cognate` | Sie wohnt allein. *("She lives alone.")* | Middle High German al-ein, literally all one — the identical compound that formed English alone (Middle English al-one). [wiktionary] | core |
| 890 | **alles** | everything; all | - | /ˈaləs/ | `Direct Cognate` | Alles ist gut. *("Everything is fine.")* | Old High German allaz, the neuter of all 'all' + -es: literally all of it. [wiktionary] | core |
| 891 | **jeder** | each; every | - | /ˈjeːdɐ/ | `th_to_d` | Jeder Tag ist anders. *("Every day is different.")* | From Old High German ioweder (io 'ever' + wedar), the compound the page compares with English either; it originally meant each of two before merging with ieder. [wiktionary] | core |
| 892 | **etwas** | something; some | - | /ˈɛtvas/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich habe etwas Wasser. *("I have some water.")* | From Old High German eddeshwaz: edde- (probably the old word behind oder 'or') + hwaz 'what' — originally something like or-what, hence something. [wiktionary] | core |
| 893 | **nichts** | nothing | - | /nɪçt͡s/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich verstehe nichts. *("I understand nothing.")* | From Middle High German nihtes niht, literally nothing of nothing — an emphatic doubling of niht 'not', just like French rien de rien. [wiktionary] | core |
| 894 | **niemand** | nobody; no one | - | /ˈniːmant/ | `Direct Cognate` | Niemand war zu Hause. *("Nobody was at home.")* | nie 'never' + Mann 'man': literally never-man — the page analyzes it exactly so, the same idea as English no man and none. [wiktionary] | core |
| 895 | **wieder** | again | - | /ˈviːdɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Wir sehen uns wieder. *("We will see each other again.")* | Old High German widar 'against', from West Germanic *wiþr 'toward, against' — the page names English wither and widdershins as cognates; German wieder turned against into again. | 896 | **echt** | real; genuine | - | /ɛçt/ | `Direct Cognate` | Das ist echt lecker. *("That is really tasty.")* | From Middle Low German echt 'lawful, genuine', from ē 'law' — related to German Ehe 'marriage', the lawful bond; English uses echt as a loan from German. [wiktionary] | core |
| 897 | **ganz** | whole; quite | - | /ɡants/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich bin ganz müde. *("I am quite tired.")* | West Germanic *gant 'whole, sound' — Middle High German ganz meant whole and healthy; no English cognate is listed on the page. [wiktionary] | core |
| 898 | **einmal** | once; one time | - | /ˈaɪ̯nmaːl/ | `Direct Cognate` | Einmal im Jahr fahre ich ans Meer. *("Once a year I go to the sea.")* | ein 'one' + Mal 'time': Middle High German einmāle — literally one-time, hence once. [wiktionary] | core |
| 899 | **einige** | some; a few | - | /ˈaɪ̯nɪɡə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Einige Leute warten vor dem Kino. *("Some people are waiting in front of the cinema.")* | Inflected form of einig, from ein (the exact twin of English one, Proto-Germanic *ainaz) plus the -ig suffix; some, a few. | 900 | **beide** | both | - | /ˈbaɪ̯də/ | `Direct Cognate` | Beide Kinder lernen Deutsch. *("Both children are learning German.")* | From Proto-Germanic *bai, the ancient Germanic dual meaning both, two; the same root grew into English both, which fused it with a demonstrative. | 901 | **sonst** | otherwise; else | - | /zɔnst/ | `Direct Cognate` | Beeil dich, sonst kommen wir zu spät. *("Hurry up, otherwise we will be too late.")* | From Old High German sus (thus), Proto-West Germanic *sus, from the ancient root *swa that also gives English so; the sense drifted from thus to otherwise. [wiktionary] | core |

| 904 | **irgendwo** | somewhere | - | /ˈɪʁɡəntˌvoː/ | `Direct Cognate` | Mein Handy ist irgendwo im Haus. *("My phone is somewhere in the house.")* | Irgend (any, at all) plus wo (where, the twin of English where); literally anywhere, that is somewhere. [wiktionary] | core |

| 916 | **außerdem** | besides; in addition | - | /ˈaʊ̯sɐdeːm/ | `Direct Cognate` | Außerdem habe ich eine Katze. *("Besides, I have a cat.")* | Außer (outside of, built on aus — out, twin of English out) plus dem (that); literally besides that. English besides is unrelated. [wiktionary] | core |

| 950 | **verdienen** | to earn | - | /fɛɐ̯ˈdiːnən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Sie verdient viel Geld. *("She earns a lot of money.")* | From ver- + dienen (to serve), a calque of Latin deservio - the same Latin verb that gave English deserve (via Old French deservir). | 951 | **Kellner** | waiter | der (m) | /ˈkɛlnɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Kellner bringt das Essen. *("The waiter brings the food.")* | From Medieval Latin cellenarius (cellarer), from Late Latin cellarium - the pantry word that also gave German Keller and English cellar. [wiktionary][etymonline] | professions |
| 952 | **Handwerker** | craftsman | der (m) | /ˈhantˌvɛʁkɐ/ | `k_to_ch` | Der Handwerker repariert die Tür. *("The craftsman repairs the door.")* | Hand (hand) + Werker (worker); Werk comes from Proto-Germanic *werką (PIE *wérǵom) - the exact ch-twin of English work. [wiktionary][etymonline] | professions |
| 953 | **Elektriker** | electrician | der (m) | /eˈlɛktʁɪkɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Der Elektriker kommt morgen. *("The electrician is coming tomorrow.")* | elektrisch (electric) + -er; elektrisch is from Neo-Latin electricus, coined by William Gilbert from Greek elektron (amber) - the stone where static electricity was first noticed; English electric is the same coinage. [wiktionary][etymonline] | professions |

| 954 | **Sekunde** | second | die (f) | /zeˈkʊndə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Warte eine Sekunde, bitte! *("Wait one second, please!")* | Borrowed from Latin secunda, short for pars minuta secunda, the second small part of the hour — the same Latin phrase behind English second. [wiktionary] | time |
| 955 | **Mittag** | midday | der (m) | /ˈmɪtaːk/ | `d_to_t` | Wir essen um zwölf Uhr zu Mittag. *("We eat lunch at twelve o'clock.")* | Mitt(e) + Tag, literally middle-day — the exact structural twin of English midday; Tag is the twin of day (d → t). [wiktionary] | time |
| 956 | **Mitternacht** | midnight | die (f) | /ˈmɪtɐnaχt/ | `y_gh_to_g_ch` | Der Film beginnt um Mitternacht. *("The film begins at midnight.")* | Mitter + Nacht — inherited from Proto-Germanic *midjanahts, the very same middle-night compound as English midnight. [wiktionary] | time |
| 957 | **Wochenende** | weekend | das (n) | /ˈvɔxənˌɛndə/ | `k_to_ch` | Am Wochenende fahren wir an den See. *("On the weekend we go to the lake.")* | Woche + Ende — the modern compound arose in the 19th century as a calque of English weekend; Woche is the twin of week (k → ch) and Ende the twin of end. | 958 | **Feiertag** | public holiday | der (m) | /ˈfaɪɐ̯taːk/ | `d_to_t` | Heute ist ein Feiertag, alles ist zu. *("Today is a public holiday, everything is closed.")* | Feier + Tag — literally celebration day; Tag is the twin of day (d → t). [wiktionary] | time |
| 959 | **Ferien** | holidays | die (f) | /ˈfeːri̯ən/ | `Direct Cognate` | Im Sommer haben wir sechs Wochen Ferien. *("In summer we have six weeks of holidays.")* | Plural-only word from Latin feriae, days of rest and festival, with the Latin plural ending replaced by German -en. [wiktionary] | time |
| 960 | **Termin** | appointment | der (m) | /tɛʁˈmiːn/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich habe morgen einen Termin beim Zahnarzt. *("I have a dentist appointment tomorrow.")* | From Latin terminus, boundary or limit — the same Latin word that gives English term and terminus. [wiktionary] | time |
| 961 | **Kalender** | calendar | der (m) | /kaˈlɛndɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Ich schreibe den Termin in meinen Kalender. *("I write the appointment in my calendar.")* | From Latin calendārium — the same Latin word that gives English calendar. [wiktionary] | time |
| 962 | **Wecker** | alarm clock | der (m) | /ˈvɛkɐ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Mein Wecker klingelt um sechs Uhr. *("My alarm clock rings at six o'clock.")* | wecken, to wake, + the agent suffix -er — literally a waker. [wiktionary] | time |
| 963 | **Anfang** | beginning | der (m) | /ˈʔanfaŋ/ | `Direct Cognate` | Am Anfang war es schwer. *("At the beginning it was hard.")* | an + Fang, catch — from anfangen, to catch at and so to begin; the entry lists archaic English fang, to seize, as a cognate. [wiktionary] | time |
| 964 | **Ende** | end | das (n) | /ˈɛndə/ | `Direct Cognate` | Am Ende des Films weine ich immer. *("At the end of the film I always cry.")* | Inherited from Proto-Germanic *andijaz — the same root as English end. [wiktionary] | time |
| 965 | **zuerst** | at first | - | /tsuˈʔeːɐ̯st/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Zuerst frühstücken wir, dann gehen wir. *("First we have breakfast, then we go.")* | zu + erst — zu is the twin of to (t → z) and erst the Germanic earliest, kept in archaic English erstwhile. [wiktionary] | time |
| 966 | **Uhrzeit** | clock time | die (f) | /ˈuːɐ̯ˌt͡saɪ̯t/ | `t_to_s_ss_z` | Weißt du die Uhrzeit? *("Do you know the time?")* | Uhr + Zeit — clock-time; Zeit is the twin of English tide, which originally meant time, as in eventide. [wiktionary] | time |
| 967 | **abends** | in the evenings | - | /ˈaːbən(t)s/ | `Direct Cognate` | Abends lese ich ein Buch. *("In the evenings I read a book.")* | Abend + adverbial -s, from an old adverbial genitive — the twin formation of the English adverb evenings. [wiktionary] | time |
| 968 | **morgens** | in the mornings | - | /ˈmɔʁɡəns/ | `Direct Cognate` | Morgens trinke ich Kaffee. *("In the mornings I drink coffee.")* | Morgen + adverbial -s, from an old adverbial genitive — the twin formation of the English adverb mornings. [wiktionary] | time |
| 969 | **nachts** | at night | - | /naχts/ | `y_gh_to_g_ch` | Nachts ist es hier ganz still. *("At night it is completely quiet here.")* | Nacht + adverbial -s — the entry lists the English adverb nights as a cognate; Nacht is night with gh → ch. [wiktionary] | time |
| 970 | **mittags** | at midday | - | /ˈmɪtaːks/ | `d_to_t` | Mittags essen wir warm. *("At midday we eat a hot meal.")* | Mittag + adverbial -s — literally at midday; Mittag is the structural twin of English midday. [wiktionary] | time |
| 971 | **Datum** | date | das (n) | /ˈdaːtʊm/ | `Direct Cognate` | Welches Datum haben wir heute? *("What is the date today?")* | Borrowed from Latin datum, literally something given — the same Latin participle that gave English date, from the Roman letter formula given at Rome. | 974 | **Mittwoch** | Wednesday | der (m) | /ˈmɪtvɔx/ | `k_to_ch` | Am Mittwoch habe ich Sport. *("On Wednesday I have sports.")* | Mitte + Woche — literally mid-week; the entry cites English midweek as the cognate compound and notes it calqued Latin media hebdomas. [wiktionary] | time |
| 975 | **Werktag** | workday | der (m) | /ˈvɛrkˌtaːk/ | `k_to_ch`, `d_to_t` | An Werktagen stehen wir früh auf. *("On workdays we get up early.")* | Werk + Tag — the exact structural twin of workday; Werk is the twin of work (k → ch) and Tag the twin of day (d → t). [wiktionary] | time |
